This is a digital copy of a book that was preserved for generations on library shelves before it was carefully scanned by Google as part of a project 

to make the world's books discoverable online. 

It has survived long enough for the copyright to expire and the book to enter the public domain. A public domain book is one that was never subject 

to copyright or whose legal copyright term has expired. Whether a book is in the public domain may vary country to country. Public domain books 

are our gateways to the past, representing a wealth of history, culture and knowledge that's often difficult to discover. 

Marks, notations and other maiginalia present in the original volume will appear in this file - a reminder of this book's long journey from the 

publisher to a library and finally to you. 

Usage guidelines 

Google is proud to partner with libraries to digitize public domain materials and make them widely accessible. Public domain books belong to the 
public and we are merely their custodians. Nevertheless, this work is expensive, so in order to keep providing tliis resource, we liave taken steps to 
prevent abuse by commercial parties, including placing technical restrictions on automated querying. 
We also ask that you: 

+ Make non-commercial use of the files We designed Google Book Search for use by individuals, and we request that you use these files for 
personal, non-commercial purposes. 

+ Refrain fivm automated querying Do not send automated queries of any sort to Google's system: If you are conducting research on machine 
translation, optical character recognition or other areas where access to a large amount of text is helpful, please contact us. We encourage the 
use of public domain materials for these purposes and may be able to help. 

+ Maintain attributionTht GoogXt "watermark" you see on each file is essential for in forming people about this project and helping them find 
additional materials through Google Book Search. Please do not remove it. 

+ Keep it legal Whatever your use, remember that you are responsible for ensuring that what you are doing is legal. Do not assume that just 
because we believe a book is in the public domain for users in the United States, that the work is also in the public domain for users in other 
countries. Whether a book is still in copyright varies from country to country, and we can't offer guidance on whether any specific use of 
any specific book is allowed. Please do not assume that a book's appearance in Google Book Search means it can be used in any manner 
anywhere in the world. Copyright infringement liabili^ can be quite severe.

I Sir David Mayne was % notary, and the luccewor of 
John Mayne in the poeaession of properties situated 
between Trongate and Bridgegate and elsewhere. (See 
protocols Nos. 78, 180, 188, 323, 3J>9-60, etc.) The name 
of the first owner has been coounemorated in the desig- 
nation of a wynd which passed through or along his 
properties, the *' vennel of John Mayn " being referred 
to in an instrument of saaine, dated 5 January, 1543 
(Liber GoUegii Nostre Domine, p. 115). The wynd still 
exists, but is one of those which the Corporation of 
Gla^owhave, by the Improvements Act of 1807, obtained 
authority to close. At the date of the above protocol, 
Stockwellgait was beyond the west port, and, conse- 
quently, the Old Wynd, other>%ise called Lindsay's 
Wester Wynd, formed the westmost opening within the 
dty, between the Trongate and the Bridgegate. Not 
many years afterwards the New or Lindsay's Easter 
Wynd is noticed in the records, but unless it was in 
existence in 1573, Maynes Wynd must have been the 
next opening between the two thoroughfares. Eastward 
of Maynes Wynd was the AUey, on each side of which 
were the New Kirk yards, transmissions of which are 
frequently referred to in the protocols. At the beginning 
of last century, these lanes were felt to be inadequate 
for increasing traffic, and, as narrated in a council 



minute, dated 25 April, 1722, "the toun, with great 
charge and expense, purchased several lands and yeards 
in the Tronegate. opposite to the Caiidlerig Street, for 
making of a cross street in the middle of the Trongate, 
from the foot of the Candlerig Street to the Bridgegate 
Street . . . and that not only for beautifying the 
city, but also for the better accommodation of the 
inhabitants and people resorting thereto, and for the 
more easie passage from one street to another . . . 
And do staiut and appoynt that the street be designed 
for hereafter by the name of Kings Street" (MS. Ck>uncil 
Records, xx. p. 496). Some of the properties acquired by 
the town council at that time were described in the 
titles deeds as bounded on the east by the Alley, and on 
the west by Maynes Wynd. The lots on each side of 
King Street were disposed of on condition that a uniform 
building plan should be adhered to, and it was stipulated 
" that the said street be 30 foot of breadth betwixt the 
strands or gutter, on each syde thereof, and 2^ foot 
furder betwixt the strands or gutters and the front of 
the houses, making in all 35 foot in breadth." {lb.) It 
was probably in consequence of its proximity to Uie new 
and spacious street that John Maynes vennel acquired 
its alternative designation of the Back Wynd.

iThe lands of Oarniyle, in the parish of Old Monkland, 
are situated on the right bank of the Olyde, about four 
miles south-east of Glasgow Cross. Under ^e ancient 
name of Kermil they appear on record, in the twelfth 
oentury, as a gift from Herbert, bishop of Glasgow 
(1147-64) to the abbey of Newbotle. Kings and Popes 
from time to time confirmed the lands to the abbey; but 
in the chartulary a note appended to the transcript of a 
papal bull, dated 1263, shows that the monks had then 
ceased to be owners (Registrum de NeuboUe— Bannatyne 
Club, pp. 91, 133, 181, 191, 816). How the change 
occurred is explained in a charter granted by John de 
Cheyam, bishop of Glasgow, on 11 June, 1268, it being 
there narrated that the bishop had, with his own money, 
and with the help of Sir Reginald of Irewyn, sometime 
archdeacon of Glasgow, purchased or redeemed the land 
of Kermil, with its pertinents, and being zealous for the 
increase of divine service in the church of Glasgow, he 
dedicated the property (excepting the new mill which 
he had erected on the water of Olyde) in pure and 
perpetual alms, for the sustenance of three chaplains or 
priests to celebrate divine service in the church of 
Glasgow, for the weal of his soul and of the souls of 
archdeacon Reginald and of all their predecessors and 
successors forever. The dean and chapter were authorised 



to appoint the chaplains when vacancies occurred, and a 
malediction was invoked on any who should violate the 
purpose of the endowment (Regrlstrum Episcopatus 
Glasguensis, pp. 178-9X Bishop John's pious arrange* 
ments, however, seem to have been disregarded by 
Robert Wischard, who succeeded to the bishopric in 
1272 ; and his interference led the dean and chapter, in 
the year 1275, to appeal to the pope for redress. The 
papal court thereupon authorised the bishops of Dunblane 
and Argyle to investigate the complaint, and some docu* 
ments relating to the Judicial procedure, but not the 
final decision, will be found in the printed Register (lb. 
pp. 183-9). That the lands ultimately reverted to the 
bishopric ii indicated by the fact that during the period 
embraced in the printed Rental Book (1610-70) the 
entries of rentallers in Carmyle, lands are somewhat 
numerous. The transfer by Armour to Stewart referred 
to in the text is apparently that entered in the Rental 
Book on 10 February, 1564-5, though the value is there 
stated at half a merk less than it is in the protocol: 
"Alexander Stewart is rentallit in xxs. land in Uver 
Garmyld, be consent and venditioun of David Armour, 
last rentellit thiUrin " (Diocesan Registers — Grampian 
Club. i. p. 183), 



f. 112.

with this provision, that if the said John, who was about to proceed to 
parts beyond the sea, happened to return in his own person (no procurator 
or assignee appointed to that effect having power to use said reversion), 
then Isabella and her spouse should renounce the property to him in common 
form of reversion. And John Cottis for himself and his heirs consented 
that, in the event of William Donaldson surviving him and Isabella Symple, 
he (Donaldson) should be paid five merks, yearly, from the rents of the 
said tenement during his lifetime.

iThis passage was perhaps the original of what is now 
called the New or Lindsay's Easter Wynd. See No. 1924. 
The Lindsays of Dunrod and Kittokside were long 
connected with Glasgow as owners of property and 
otherwise. Robert Lindsay of Dunrod was provost of 
Glasgow in 1561-2 (Protoool No. 683). The lands cif 
Kittokside, near Carmunnock, were confirmed by King 
Robert II. to John of Lyndesay of Dunrod on 12 
November, 1383 (Reg. Mag. Sig. i. p. 167, No. 88) ; and 
by King James IV. to David Lindesay, grandion of 
Alexander Lindesay of Dunrod, on 18 April, 1502 
{Tb. it No. 2610), and there were subsequent ooDflnna- 



tions to members of the family ; but about the yev 
1620 Alexander Lyndsay of Dunrod disposed of the 
property to James Hammiltoun of Peill (76. viii. No. 
719). According to the " Ordnance Gazetteer,** Dunrod 
was an ancient barony in the parish of Inverkip, 
Renfrewshire, which belonged to the Lindsays from the 
time of King Robert the Bruce till the year 1619 ; but 
in crown charters the lands are described not aa a 
barony but as the 10 merk land, old extent, of Dunrod, 
with tower, manor place, grain and fulling mills, etc 
(i&. vii. No. 2023 ; vUl. No. 726). Archibald Stewart of 
Ardgowane was the purchaser in 1619.

2017. Cochrane. 25 February, 1573-4, 8 a.m. 

Michael Barde, procurator and in name of John Steward of Mynto, 
knight, resigned in favour of David Cochrane, two upper merchant booths, then 
occupied by John Wylie and Patrick Glen, merchants, lying discontiguous, 
one being part of a great fore tenement, built by q. Mr. James Houston, 
subdean of Glasgow, on the west side of the street leading from the 
market cross to the South Port, between the lands of Mathew Wilsoun 



miller's house. That when prelacy was suppressed in 
Scotland it was the practice of the crown to grant feu 
rights to the kindly tennents and possessors of their 
temporality or lands helongintr to them. That the 
rentallere in the bishoprick of Glasgow ha%e been 
adjudged to have a right perpetual, of the nature of a 
copyhold, and for that reason, upon application, feu 
rights were never denyd them. That this city has not 
had any profitable bargain of this rental, the rent being 
very high, and were willing to have surrendered their 
right, and under form of instrument tendered the keys 
of the milln in the court of exchequer, while Baron 
Smith presided in the exchequer, in order to be freed of 
the milln duty, but the court refused to accept of the 
surrender, and declared that they would subject the 
city, nevertheless, to the payment of the accustomed 
duty, unless an exemption was procured by grant from 
the crown. That this city have been so ancient kindly 
possessors and rentallers they are willing to continue in 
the payment of the duty ; and, in order to enable them 
to make the milln (which at present is a burden upon 
them) of some profit to the city, and to the country in 



general, to lay out money in the improvement thereof, 
if his Majesty be pleased to grant a feu right of the said 
milln and others for payment of the usual duty in name 
of feu.farm" (MS. Council Record, xxiii. p. 423). The 
desire of the mi^strates and council was o<Mnplied with 
and a crown charter was granted in their favour on 
8 July, 1738 (MS. Inventory of Writs, U. p. 130, b. 26). 
The mill was retained by the city till 1809, when it was 
sold "to John Gibson, Robert Shaw, Thomas Douglas, 
and John Douglas ... in consideration of the sum 
of £400 stg., and payment and performance of the sums, 
victual, rent, and services mentioned in the Disposition ** 
(MS. Ck)unoil Record, xliv. p. 96.) It Is understood that 
the Bishop Grain Mills, at 206 Old Dumbarton Road, on 
the left bank of the Kelvin, now occupy the site of the 
old Partick Mill. Mr. James White, Joint proprietor of 
the existing mills, has prepared a paper to be read before 
the Glasgow Archnological Society, narrating the early 
history of the property, and to that paper, when it 
appears in the Society's proceedings, reference may be 
made for further particulars.

to his Jurisdiction, from whence he might issue brieves 
to his bailie for the service of heirs ; and the service 
proceeding on such brief, when recorded in the boolu 
of the regality, was as effectual as a retour on a brieve 
issuing from the King's ctiancery" (Institute Ut. iv. 
8. 7). By a decision on 8 December, 1631, the court 
of session sustidned a service before the bailie of 
the regality of Dumfermling, " who has their own 
ehappelf and so it was not necessary to be retoured to 
the King's chancellary" (Durie, p. 606). The charter 
by King James II. in 1450, erecting the regality of 
Glasgow, contains an express grant of chapel— cum

(Glasgow Charters, iL pp. 30, 62, etc.) See No. 1314. 

1 One of the heads of a brieve of service required the 
inquest, in compliance with the sti^ute James IIL, 
1474, o. 10, to report "quhat the land was of availl of 
the anld, and the reiy availl that it is worthe and gives 
the day of the serving of the said breif." The eariier 
valuation — supposed to be that of the time of King 
Alexander III. — was called the old extent, and the 
other valuation the new extent. One purpose of the 
"new extent" was the ascertainment of the amount 
payable by vassals to their superiors on entry, while 
sometimes the one and sometimes the other extent was 
used as the basis of national taxation. It may here be 
interesting to quote what is said by Sir John Skene, 
lord clerk register, on the subject of extents for retour 
purposes, especially as his remarlcs were penned shortly 
after the date of Uie protocol : — " Extent of landes 
signifies the rents, profltesand issues of the SMnin ,quhairof 
there is twa kindes, the auld extent and the new 
extent. For it appeoris that the rentall and valour of 
landes has been taxed and Uquidat to ane oertaine 
summe of silver, conforme to the profltes and dewties 
quhilk the landes payed at that time quhilk is called 
the auld and first extent tempore paci$. Bot because 



the revenues and dewties of lands be progrtm of time 
did increase and grow mair and mair, ane other 
taxatioun and extent was maid in the time of peace, as 
the former extent, conforme to the profites augmented, 
quhilk therefore is called the new or second extent." It 
will be observed that both old and new valuations apply 
to the " time of peace," in regard to which Skene offers 
this explanation : — " For tempore heUi^ or in time of 
weire, there is na ordinar or certain extent of landes 
prescrived be ony law ; for in sik time, either the lands 
are waste and are not laboured, or tiie lands-lordes 
servis in proper persone, and it is na reason that they 
suld baith make personal service, and also pay extent 
or taxation." Skene states that the lords of session had 
in 1641 held that the new extent should be quadruple 
the old, and suggests that this rule '* suld be generalhe 
used in retouring of landes to the King's ohoncellarie 
and uthers chappelles, albeit the samin is nocht 
perpetuallie observed." ( Skene's De Verborum 
Signiflcatione, voce Extent). The printed Retoun of 
the Services of Heirs (od CapeUam Domini Regie) 
show great variety in the proportions between the two 
valuations, and it is probable that at first, instead of a 
fixed ratio, each case was Judged on its own merits. 
Latterly, it was the praotioe not to make a new 
valuation on the occasion of a new inquest, but merely 
to adopt the valuation contained in a previous retour. 
In oases, also, where lands were held in feu, the fen* 
duty was inserted as the " new extent," because it was 
this which determined the amount of the superior's 
claims. For these reasons the " new extent,** as given 
in retoun, cannot always be relied on as showing the 
actual value of lands at the date of inquisition. In the 
case of Hillok, mentioned in the text, old and new 
extent were identical, probably because the lands had 
not long formed a separate holding and there was no 
record of any other valuation.

1 This ditch was probably the b^^ning of the Gyrth 
Bum, a smaU streamlet which Joined the Malyndoner, 
near Drygate (see protocol No. 018, p. 107). The course 
of the burn, now a covered sewer, is shown on a map of 
the city and suburbs, originally published by Mr. 
Fleming, in 1807, and surveyed and brought down to 
1821, by David Smith. (Commencing in the vicinity of 
the present Hopetoun Place, where it was about 13 yards 
south of Stirlings Road, and 00 yards north of Rottenrow, 
the burn held eastward, in a curving line, passing 
through the site of the old gasworks, about 60 feet 
south of St. Nicholas Street, and 86 yards north of 
Rottenrow, In 1783, the roadwi^ at the intersection of 



High Street and Rottenrow was lowered four feet 
(Oleland's Annals, 1829, p. 478), but previous to that 
alteration and other levelling the northward street 
crossed a deep hollow, through which the Gyrth Bum 
found its way to the Malyndoner. In a list of common 
sewers constructed within the royalty previous to 1817, 
Oleland specifies one "formed in the hollow ground 
a little to the souUi of Stirlings Road," and running 
eastward to Taylor Street (lb. p. 478X It was early 
the custom to convert burns into sewers, and, there- 
fore, it may be assumed tiuX the old ditch was

I The charter was oonflrmed by Uie crown on 28 
March, 1576 (Reg. Mag. Sig. iv. No. 2407X As there 
quoted, the Bishop's charter bears to have been granted 
In consideration of the great expenses and labours of 
Lord Bo}^! in upholding the inrivilege of the regality, 
and his thorough administration of justice, which for 
muiy years had been neglected ; and it was provided 
that the bailie should render to the archbishop the 
senrioes due in respect of the office, and defend the 
archbisbc^ric in all its lawful concerns. At the annual 
municipal election in October following his appointment 
to the bailliary. Lord Boyd was nominated for the 
pro?ostship, by a commission under the hand of the 
archbnhop. That document narrates " the babilite and 
qualiflcatioun of aue noble lord Robert Lord Boyd, 
bailie of our baroue of Olasgu, in ministratioun of Justice 
wrychtlie to all persounes," and states " that the office 
(tf provestre of the brucht and ciete of Qlasgw hes nevir 
or seyndill (i,e. seldom) bene separatit in sindry 
persounes handis fra ihe baillierie of cure baronie 
f oirsaid " (Glasg. Rec. L p. 22). This reference to the 
offices ot bailie of the regality and provost of the 
burgh being usually combined in one person may have 
been apjdicable to the depute but not to the principal 
bailie, a conjunction which, so far as is known, only 
occurred in 1610-1, when the earl of Lennox was provost. 

The barony of Glasgow and a large discrict towards 
the east, known as Bishopforest, were erected into a 
regality under a charter granted by King James IL to 
the bishop and his successors, dated 20 April, 1460. The 
ludding oi courts and the exercise of c^her privil^^ 



usually appertaining to regalities were conferred by the 
charter ; and these, according to the institutional writers, 
included the power of appointing bailies possessing 
heritable jurisdiction. Erskine sa^s: " It was singular 
in this high jurisdiction that the lord of regality might 
appoint deputies, called steward* or bailies^ not only 
during pleasure or for life, but heritable, who had, by 
that deputation, all the proflts incident to the Juris- 
diction made over in perpeluum to themselves and their 
heirs," (Institute of the Law of Scotland, B. I., t. iv., s. 7). 
No record of an appointment to the Glasgow bailliary 
in the 15th century is known to be extant, but it 
is believed that for a long period the earls of Lennox 
held the office. In proceedings before the privy 
council on 14 May, 1578, it is stated t^at Mathew 
earl of Lennox (the R^^nt) had been bailie, and 
that "his guidsire, grandsire, foirgrandsire, and all 
utheris his foirbearis, wer kyndlie baillies of the 
samyn (i.e., they possessed the office by 'kyndlie' 
tenure), and broukit the office thoirof past all memory" 
(Privy Ck>uncil Reg. ii. p. 607X During the time Earl 
Mathew was in exile (1545^i) the Earl of Arraii held 
the appointment, but on the restoration of Lennox to 
royal favour in 1564, he was reinstated in the office 
{lb. i. p. 291. Glasgow Charters I. pp. IxxxL, IxxxviL, 
dxl. ; II. p. 125). On being promoted to the regency, 
Lennox could not conveniently exercise the office, and 
he accordingly devolved it on Sir John Steward of 
Mynto. In the proceedings of the privy council already 
referred to (14 May, 1578), it was represented on behalf 
of King James VI., grandson and heir of the R^ent,

a It has been stated by historians and biographers 
that Mr. John Davidson, principal regent of the college, 
was a different person from Mr. John Davidson, minister 
of Hamilton, but there is good reason to believe that it 
was the same individual who filled these offices. Regent 
Davidson is supposed to have belonged to the north of 



Scotland, and to have been born about 1520. Educated for 
the church, he passed some time at Paris University, and 
returned to this country about 1556. On IS March, 
1556-7, he is designated *' Master Johne Davidsone, 
pensionar of the personage of Kinkell, wythin the 
diocesy of Aberdeine, prinoipall regent of the said (i.^,, 
Glasgow) Universite and Pedagoge thairof " (Munimenta 
i. p. 61X After the Reformation, Davidson for some time 
adhered to the old faith, but eventually joined the 
protestant party. In 1563, he published "Ane answer 
to the Compendious Tractive of Mr. Quintin Kennedy, 
abbot of Crossragael," a controversi^ Pi^per which has 
been reprinted by the Wodrow Society (Miscellany, p. 
177X with a biographical sketch by Dr. David Laing. 
After 1572 his identification as "principal regent" 
ceases. 

"Mr. John Davidson, minister of Hamilton,'* is found 
in the Register of Ministers for the year 1567, possessing 
a stipend of £120 ; and he is traced in General Assembly 
proceedings and elsewhere as incumbent of that parish 
till near the close of the century. Dr. Laing expressly 
states that the "minister" was not the "principal 
regent"; while Dr. M'Crie, in his life of Andrew 
Melville, assumes that Davidson had died before 
Melville was installed his successor. The facts narrated 
in protocols 2112 and 2113 are, however, inconsistent 
with these views. 

After the change of religion, the university appears to 
have been deserted by the beneficed clergy connected 
with it ; and " among other losses and misfortunes of 
our city," it was perceived that the schools and colleges 
were " wholly going to ruin " (Glasgow (Charters, ii. p. 
140). The town councU and community thereupon 
came to the rescue and, re-endowing the oollege with 
the ecclesiastical revenues which they had obtained 
under Queen Mary's charter, made provision for the 
maintenance of a prindpal, two regents, and twelve

Sir John Skene, in De Verbonun Sigmficatione, explains

reason of heretage, or conquest in the time of his first

time of his second wife saU perteine to the baime gotten 
with her in the second marriage."

to Bain's Calendar of Documents relating to Scotland, 
il. No0. 1457, 1084; iii. Noe. SO, 245, 461. On 22 
February, 1303-4, at an inquisition before the sheriff of 
Stirling, it was found that the lands of "Galentir" 
Callander) were " worth yearly in time of peace, £40, 
and now £8 6& 8d.," and that the Undsof " Kelynsithe" 
(Kilsyth) were "worth yearly in time of peace, £60, and 
at present £12." In 1306-7, Uuwe de Cham pane petitioned 
the English king and his council for mitigation of 
certain dums for his lands in Galloway, asking that 
these should be settled on the basis of the "present 
value, not the old valuation before the Scottinh war, as 
they have been so wasted thereby." The deliverance 



authorised the chamberlain to settle " either by a new^ 
extent or by the old register." It seems evident that 
in the cases cited the "old extent" was the valuation 
previous to the English wars which followed the death 
of King Alexander III. ; but see Mr. Thomas Thomson's

1 M'Ure, in his History of Glasgow, mentions that he 
was a descendant of these spouses, and gives a lengthy 
aooount of the Lyon family. His preliminary remarks 
may be quoted :— "Archibald Lyon, lawful son of the 
deeeas'd lord Glames, thereafter earl of Strathmore, who 
came to Glasgow with Gavin Dunbar, late arohbishc^ 
thereof, in the [16th] century, whose ofl^ring is 
collected from several documents, contracts of marriage, 
and oUier evidences belonging to them, and extracted 
furth thereof by the author, who was great-grandchild 
to the said Mr. Archibald Lyon. The said Mr. Archibald 
Lyon having resided a long time in the ouBtle of Glasgow 
with the archbishop, he married Margaret Dunlop, a 
daughter of the funily thereof, and thereafter became 
merchant here, and undertook great adventures and 
voyages in trading to Poland, France, and Holland, his 
endeavours were wonderfully blessed with success, 
whereby he acquired considerable lands in and about 
the dty of Glasgow. He built a great lodging for him- 
self and family, about 200 years ago, upon the south side 
of the Gallowgate Street, whereupon his arms and lady's 
are yet to be seen upon the gate of his lodging to tills 
very day. Thereafter he built four dosses of houses and 
forty-four shops, high and low, on the south side of the 
Gallowgate and a part of the east side of the Saltmarket. 
He lived till he attained to the age of ninety-five 
years " (M'Ure's History, 1830 edition, pp. 101-2). What 
is here said about the noble parentage of Archibald 
Lyon is not consistent with the usual accounts of the 
Glammis and Strathmore family ; but with regard to 
the acquisition of properties in the vicinity of Gallowgate 
and Saltmarket the protocols afford confirmatory 
evidence. The property mentioned in the text was 
probably part of land in " Eglissemis Oroft," acquired 



by Archibald Lyon, in 1560 (protocol. No. 550) ; and 
as it was bounded on the north by lands already 
belonging to him, the latter must have bordered on the 
Gallowgate. 

2 Eagleshame or "Eglissimeis** Croft formed the 
north-western comer of what is supposed to have beoi 
Oondud, a 12th century possession of. the see of 
Glasgow. Adjoining portions of these lands bad been 
bestowed on St. Nicholas Hospital, the vicars of the 
choir and certain ohaplainries ; and it may be oon- 
Jectured that the croft itsdf had been at one time an 
endowment of the rector of Eagleshame, the south- 
eastmoet parish in Renfrewshire, thereby acquiring 
its designation. **Eglisham" first appears on record 
in a charter by King Malcolm IV. (confirming a 
grant by King David) to Walter the Steward in 1167-8. 
From the high steward the estate passed to the Mont- 
gomery family ; and about the year 1430, Sir Alexander 
Montgomery consented to the church being erected by 
Bishop Oameron into a prebend for a canon of Glasgow 
cathedral (Origines Parochialies, i. pp. 99, 508. Reg. 
Episc. Glasg. No. 840X If the croft was really assigned 
to the parson of Eagleshame, he appears to have conveyed 
it in small lots to sevenJ purchasers or rentallers. In 
1490, Martin Wan, chancellor of the metropolitan diurch, 
gave to a chaplainry an acre of land in the croft 
"commonly called EgUssamezcrof t " (76. No. 473); 
while James Houstoun, founder of the collegiate churdi 
of St. Mary and St. Anne, acquired other portions of 
the croft which were induded among the endowments 
of his church in 1548 (Liber Ecdesie Gollegiate, pp. 101, 
112X There are likewise several protocols narrating 
transfers of lots in the croft.

1 Am shown by crown charten, the lands of Fulbar 
and Ser^eantlaw, in Paisley parish, belonged for a 
long time to saooessive generations of the Hall family. 
When Crawford wrote his History of Renfrewshire 
(puUished in 1710), they were still proprietors, and it is 
stated that they had settled there at least as early as 
tiie year 1870. The possessions of the family in the 
burgh of Glasgow can likewise be traced back for a 
considerable period. In Munimenta FrtUrttm Pre- 
dicatorumt pubUshed by the Maitland Clnb (pp. 105-8X 
there is ininted an Indenture, dated 25 June, 1485, 
whereby " Bobyne of Hall of the Fulbare " gave certain 
annualrents from his Glasgow properties to the prior 
and convent of the Friars Preachers of Gla^ow for 
masses to be said at St. Katrine's altar, in the " Freyr 



Kyrk," for the souls of himself and his relatives. Special 
directions were given with regard to the ceremonies and 
the ringing of "Sanct Mungowis bell," while the 
offldatiog Friars were each to have '* a galloun of gud 
ayll (witht brede and cheee), to their collacion." 

The transfer of the orchard narrated in protocol 2145 
appears to have been preliminary to its disposal, in lots, 
as described in 2147. A tower and fortalice had stood 
hitherto on the grounds (see protocols 80 and 015), the 
buildings being doubtless used as the town mansion of 
the lairds of Fulbar. 

''Foulbar" appears hi Blaeu's Atlas (LeOi), but in 
modem maps its place is taken by '*Foxbar," to which 
the name seems to have been transformed.

2243. Chalmeris — Murrayk. 24 December, 1575, 1 p.m. 

Walter Waware and Margaret Buchquhannane, spouses, acknowledged that 



1 Each of the two principal Mendicant Orders— the 
Dominicans (Friars Preachers or Blaokfriars) .and 
Franciscans (Fnara Minors or Oreyfriars)— had a place 
in Glasgrow, but wliile the history of the former is 
comparatively well known, few particulars r^^rding the 
place of the Oreyfriars are extant. The Friars Preachers 
were established in Gla^ow as early as the reigrn of 
Alexander II., and by the time of the Reformation they 
were owners not only of extensive gardens on both sides 
of the Molendinar Burn, but also of properties in other 
parts of the city and elsewhere. The bulk of the 
endowments was transferred to Qlasfpow college, which 
thus came into possession of many title deeds and Merits 
relating to the Friars and their properties. These 
muniments were published by the Maitland Club in 
1846, with a luminous introduction from the pen of Dr. 
Joseph Robertson. 

The Grey Friars do not appear to have been settled in 
Glasgow till the reign of King James III., who, by a 
charter dated 21 December, 1479, confirmed to the 
"Friars Minors" the sites which they possessed in 
Edinburgh, St. Andrews, Aberdeen, and Glasgow (R.M.8. 
ii. No. 1434). The Glasgow site is there stated to have 
been gifted by John, bishop of Glasgow, with consent of 
his chapter, and by Mr. Thomas Fersithe, rector of 
Olai^^w. Bishop John's episcopate extended from 
1473 to 1483, so that the monastery must have been 
founded between 1473 and 1479. It will be observed from 
the text, as well as from protocol 1217, that the land on 
the west side of the monastery belonged to the rector or 
parson of Glasgow, and therefore it is not improbable 
that the site was taken from part of the parsonage 
lands, thus accounting for Fersithe being a party to the 
foundation. There is very little on record regarding the 
history of the Grey Friars In Gla^ow. Adhering to their 
original vow of poverty, they do not seem to have been 
possessed of any extraneous property, and consequently 
had no title deeds. Nor is there much to be got from 
other sources. In the year 1539, Jerome Russell, a Grey 



Friar, was burnt in Glasgow for " heresy," but it does 
not seem to be dear that he belonged to the city. (See 
works of John Knox, edited by Dr. Laing, i. pp. ^-6) 
Though the first notice tiiat has been found of the 
breaking up of the monastery is dated 19 June, 1560 (see 
protocol 1370) it is not improbable that the buildings 
were demolished, and the friars dispersed, in the summer 
or autumn of 1559, when such outbreaks were general 
throughout the country. Ck>nventions of the lords of 
the congregation were intermittently held in the dty in 
August and subsequent months ; and in March, 1559-60, 
there was skirmishing between the proteetants and the 
French auxiliaries (Bain's Calendar of Scottish Papers, 
1898, i. pp. 239-68, 336). In February, 1561-2, the privy 
ooundl authorised Glasgow and other burghs to tjp^pvo- 
priate the places which had been pr«8eesed by Friars 
(Privy Council Reg. i. p. 202); and by Queen Mary's 
charter of 1566-7 the whole possessions of the Franctsoans 
in Glasgow were conveyed to the magistrates and oounoil 
(Glasg. Charters, I p. 132X John Stewart of Mjnto who, 
according to the above protocol, owned the place of the 
Oreyfriars in 1575, probably acquired it, as he did some 
of the property of the coll^pate church of St. Mary, in 
return for payment of rent or feu-duty ; and if so, the 
college would become entitied to the annual revenue 
when the church possessions were transferred to that 
body in 1572-3 {lb. p. 149). In an early college rental of 
church properties tiiere is a charge of 40 s., pajrable by 
"John Conyugham for the Gray Freir yaird," and a note 
to the effect that in 1614 " this yaird was agmentit 
viij 11'' (Munimenta i. p. 173). 

In the Plan appended to Vol. I. of Gla^fow Protocols 
the place of the Oreyfriars is represented as lying 
between the High Street and the vennel sometime 
called Oreyfriars Wynd and now Shuttle Street The 
actual site appears to have been west of the vennel.

master Adam Wallace, George Herbertsoun, Johne Wilsoun, Cuthbert 



1 On 14 AuKUst, 1674, " Walter Graye, writtar and 
notare," was made a burgess and freeman of the city of 
Ola^ow, and his " fynes wes remitted to him gratis, be 
the baillies and counsale, for his service done and to be 
d(Hie to the commone eflaris of the towne " (Glasg. Reo. 
i. p. 19). Oraye was admitted a notary on 80 Jane, 1659, 
as narrated in protood No. 1847. 

3 The court of "perambulation of marches" was 
hdd at "the Symmerhill" on Sunday, 20 June, 1674. 
At this meeting, and likewise at another held at the 
same (dace on Sunday, 24 June, 1676, the magistrates, 
council and community passed various statutes affecting 
the commonweal of the burgh (Ola^. Roc. i. pp. 16, 62X 
It may have been on account of these gatherings, of 
unknown but probably ancient origin, having been held 
there at midsummer, that the hill got its name. 

Sunday appears to have been the usual day for 
assembling, but in 1690 " the raid of the Somerhill" was 
postponed to a Tuesday, ii June, " for the observatioun 
of the Sabbath di^ " {lb. p. 162X Weaponshawmgs, 



which by the act of parliament, 1640, c. 21, were 

appointed to be held in June and October yearly, were 

occasionally combined with the day's proceedings. Thus, 

on 21 March, 1677-8, the magistrates and council 

"appoyntit thair wi4>ynsohawing, according to tiiair 

statute, to be on the day of Symmerhill nixt " (lb. p. 67) ; 

and on 18 June, 1601, it was ordered that the "haUl 

inhabitantis, fremen, burgessis, salbe in redynes with 

thair armour, on futte, upoun Weddinsdaye morning, 

being the Symmerhill daye, and the provest, bailleis, 

and counsale, and deikines, to be on horsbak" (lb. p. 223). 

By the above protocol Symmerhill ia identified as part 

of the Gowcaddens lands. It frequently appears in the 

town's rental as let for grazing, but in the year 1666 the 

magistrates and council appointed "the gerse of the 

Simerhill, and the lone that passes to the Woodsyd, not 

to be sett heiref ter, bot to lye for the use of the towne 

kye allanerlie, and non others to deame right thairto." 

{lb. ii. p. 316). The precise locality, and subsequent 

history of the hill, have yet to be traced.
